1729: 4.3    Gaussian reference and optical covariance
1730: This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
1731: and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1732: 1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
1733: 2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
1734: The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
1735: gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
1736: inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
1737: activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

1611: shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
1612: an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
1613: in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
1614: exponential constant depending on N .

1729: 4.3    Gaussian reference and optical covariance
1730: This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
1731: and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1732: 1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
1733: 2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
1734: The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
1735: gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
1736: inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
1737: activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

1:  Yang–Mills Existence, Mass Gap, and Nontriviality
2: for Fixed Compact Simple Gauge Groups via Karcher
3:       Blocking and Exact Haar–Pivot Matching
4:                                              Harold D. Kirk III1,2,∗
5:                          1
6:                              University of Memphis, Memphis, Tennessee 38152, USA
7:                                         2
8:                                             ORCID: 0009-0000-6014-8451

10:                                                 August 3, 2026

13:                                                      Abstract
14:         Let G be a fixed compact connected simple Lie group and let ρ be a fixed faithful finite-dimensional
15:     unitary representation. We construct four-dimensional pure G Yang–Mills theory as a zero-spacing,
16:     infinite-volume limit of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures. The Wilson plaquette function
17:     is normalized at the identity to the background quadratic form used to define the coupling u = g 2 .
18:     A weighted forty-nine-path Karcher block, complete sixteen-alias normal section, charged chart-
19:     complement partition, and permanent rooted domain produce a uniform scale-two map on a centered
20:     invariant graph,
21:                                                  11 CA (G) log 2
22:                                u(2L) = u(L) +                    u(L)2 + OG (u(L)3 ).
23:                                                      24π 2
24: 
25:     The complete linear block certificate is independent of color and tensors with Ig ; all nonlinear
26:     constants are finite and may depend on G. At the physical handoff, a group-dependent product
27:     Sobolev order 2sG > dG /2 gives a Peter–Weyl Fourier–Wiener landing for the exact one-pivot and
28:     multipivot densities. Separated Haar pivots contract nonvacuum compact flux, optical covariance
29:     contracts normal-field stretches, and all remaining vertices retain their support charge. A single
30:     marked replica–BKAR forest then gives a boundary-to-center two-root connector and one physical
31:     clustering rate. General invariant-jet transport, displacement and stress Ward identities, finite
32:     dimension-four mixing, and a dimension-six rotation regulator yield a tempered, reflection-positive,
33:     translation-continuous, O(4)-covariant Schwinger family. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction gives a
34:     positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with unique vacuum and a strictly positive Hamiltonian
35:     gap. Endpoint-safe curvature-source transport produces a nonzero local vector with positive spectral
36:     mass in a finite interval above the gap. The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet
37:     observable theory. No estimate is asserted uniformly over the class of compact simple groups.

91: 1     The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary
92: 1.1    Problem and result
93: The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4 satisfying
94: the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1].
95: The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content,
96: and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction.
97:    Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U (Vρ )
98: of dimension dρ , and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color
99: factor is denoted CA (G). The Wilson plaquette function is
100:                                                         1
101:                                                                     
102:                                    wG,ρ (U ) = cρ 1 −     Re Tr ρ(U ) ,
103:                                                        dρ
104: where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the
105: background-field coupling coordinate u = g 2 . This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare
106: Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register.
107: Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed
108: pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with
109: microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant
110: curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4 .
111:    The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation
112: continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a
113: positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆ (G) > 0 such that
114:                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆ (G).
115: On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is
116:                                             11 CA (G) log 2
117:                            u(2L) = u(L) +                   u(L)2 + OG (u(L)3 ),
118:                                                  24π 2
119: with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains
120: a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆ (G), M⋆ (G)].
121: 1.2    Fixed-group conventions and finite register
122: Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants
123: use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary
124: condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local
125: theory on the contractible space R4 . The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G ,
126: Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In
127: curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces
128: are written Tr ρ(·). Let                     

134: order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite
135: ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet
136: dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume,
137: directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family,

7380: The cellwise shell series and its first two g derivatives converge with one uniform majorant in cutoff,
7381: volume, preceding weak depth and the chosen weak scale r. Consequently the derivative bounds for the
7382: total separated functional extend to crossing-cell boundaries by finite-volume C 2 continuity.

7384: Proof. Work first at finite directed depth M and inside one crossing cell of Definition A.6. Apply the
7385: exact marked conditional forest. If the top marked ancestor is a weak conditional vertex of scale j,
7386: Lemma A.14 gives the complete row at that scale. Every later weak transfer before the selected terminal
7387: factor is unmarked, and Theorem A.13 contracts the entire complete rooted packet in one norm. A
7388: coupling mark after the two source roots have joined is not omitted: it becomes another distinguished
7389: leaf and moves the top marked ancestor to its own scale.
7390:    If the top marked ancestor is the selected terminal factor, apply Theorem A.22. On a crossing cell
7391: this is the complete compact handoff packet, with the two differences created at the first-crossing scale
7392: and the remaining separation paid by the joint Haar–optical connector. On the no-crossing cell it is
7393: the finite-depth weak terminal packet on the fixed coarse torus. The latter tends to zero through two
7394: g derivatives as M → ∞. Thus no bridge-free expansion of the residual grounded covariance and no
7395: massless Schur row is required.

7398: polynomial times 2−2k eC k , independent of M and of the crossing cell. The sum of the shell bounds
7399: is uniform up to the closure of each crossing cell. The cellwise shell series and its first two derivatives
7400: therefore pass to the cutoff and volume limits uniformly on that closed cell. At a shared boundary the
7401: original total finite-volume normalized functional is C 2 by Lemma 10.3; equality of the two one-sided
7402: total derivatives passes to the limit. Thus no individual shell coefficients are compared and the crossing
7403: index is never differentiated. At g = 0 use the no-crossing cell for every M and let M → ∞.
7404:    The shell majorant is summable in k. Dominated convergence therefore permits the directed-depth,

492: Lemma 2.8 (Finite-cell analytic atlas and uniform Cauchy bounds). Let P49 be the finite path atlas
493: for one retained cell and its finite aligned-reflection template family. There exist fixed radii 0 < η0 < η1 ,
494: uniform under the standing convention, such that the product exponential, multiplication, inversion,
495: logarithm, and Karcher-mean maps defining every template extend holomorphically from Tη0 to Tη1 .
496:    Write the canonically rescaled local block as
497: 
498:                              B(x) = Lx + N (x),       N (0) = 0,             DN (0) = 0.
499: 
500: For every exponential kernel rate 0 < m < mP and r = 2, 3, there is a finite constant
501: 
502:                                              CN,r (m; η0 , η1 ) < ∞
503: 
504: such that
505:                                     sup ∥Dr N (x)∥Km ≤ CN,r (m; η0 , η1 ),
506:                                    x∈Tη0
507: 
508: uniformly under the standing convention. The same constants, enlarged by a finite maximum, serve the
509: aligned-reflection templates.
510: Proof. For one retained cell the block depends on finitely many g coordinates. The exponential and
511: multiplication maps are entire, while inversion and the principal logarithm are holomorphic on a fixed
512: neighborhood of the identity. At the flat field the Karcher critical point has positive Hessian on the
513: retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic
514: Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are
515: finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional
516: complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly
517: bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through
518: order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0 .
519:    The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed
520: number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the
521: finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted
522: Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak
523: depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum.

1394: Lemma 3.29 (History compatibility after exact output formation). At every RG step, form the complete
1395: connected output first, assign its canonical reported hull, and only then compute its regulator allocation
1396: and history certificate. Under this rule:
1397:    (i) every input history has one output owner;
1398:   (ii) no input allocation is copied to two incompatible output histories;
1399:  (iii) concatenation of successive certificates is associative;
1400:  (iv) summing over all certificates recovers the exact unlabelled connected output.
1401: Proof. For one step, canonical collect is a partition of the finite set of labelled connected histories by the
1402: least rooted hull in the fixed ordering. Thus there is one owner and the unlabelled sum is unchanged.

1394: Lemma 3.29 (History compatibility after exact output formation). At every RG step, form the complete
1395: connected output first, assign its canonical reported hull, and only then compute its regulator allocation
1396: and history certificate. Under this rule:
1397:    (i) every input history has one output owner;
1398:   (ii) no input allocation is copied to two incompatible output histories;
1399:  (iii) concatenation of successive certificates is associative;
1400:  (iv) summing over all certificates recovers the exact unlabelled connected output.
1401: Proof. For one step, canonical collect is a partition of the finite set of labelled connected histories by the
1402: least rooted hull in the fixed ordering. Thus there is one owner and the unlabelled sum is unchanged.
1403: The output allocation is a function of the already collected output gradient and complex Hessian, not of
1404: an input branch. Induct on the number of steps. At the next step the complete preceding certificate
1405: is treated as one input label, so a new canonical hull is appended once. This proves associativity and
1406: excludes copying. Forgetting the labels at every stage gives the exact iterated conditional logarithm by
1407: Fubini and the connected exponential formula.

1394: Lemma 3.29 (History compatibility after exact output formation). At every RG step, form the complete
1395: connected output first, assign its canonical reported hull, and only then compute its regulator allocation
1396: and history certificate. Under this rule:
1397:    (i) every input history has one output owner;
1398:   (ii) no input allocation is copied to two incompatible output histories;
1399:  (iii) concatenation of successive certificates is associative;
1400:  (iv) summing over all certificates recovers the exact unlabelled connected output.
1401: Proof. For one step, canonical collect is a partition of the finite set of labelled connected histories by the
1402: least rooted hull in the fixed ordering. Thus there is one owner and the unlabelled sum is unchanged.
1403: The output allocation is a function of the already collected output gradient and complex Hessian, not of
1404: an input branch. Induct on the number of steps. At the next step the complete preceding certificate
1405: is treated as one input label, so a new canonical hull is appended once. This proves associativity and
1406: excludes copying. Forgetting the labels at every stage gives the exact iterated conditional logarithm by
1407: Fubini and the connected exponential formula.

1394: Lemma 3.29 (History compatibility after exact output formation). At every RG step, form the complete
1395: connected output first, assign its canonical reported hull, and only then compute its regulator allocation
1396: and history certificate. Under this rule:
1397:    (i) every input history has one output owner;
1398:   (ii) no input allocation is copied to two incompatible output histories;
1399:  (iii) concatenation of successive certificates is associative;
1400:  (iv) summing over all certificates recovers the exact unlabelled connected output.
1401: Proof. For one step, canonical collect is a partition of the finite set of labelled connected histories by the
1402: least rooted hull in the fixed ordering. Thus there is one owner and the unlabelled sum is unchanged.
1403: The output allocation is a function of the already collected output gradient and complex Hessian, not of
1404: an input branch. Induct on the number of steps. At the next step the complete preceding certificate
1405: is treated as one input label, so a new canonical hull is appended once. This proves associativity and
1406: excludes copying. Forgetting the labels at every stage gives the exact iterated conditional logarithm by
1407: Fubini and the connected exponential formula.

1315: score. Leibniz distributes at most three marks, producing the fourth power in (2). Finally, replacing dγX
1316: by a normalized one-core reference changes only the single-core vertices and normalization scores. Their
1317: assumed upper/lower and score bounds are paid per vertex and do not alter the cross-link mass. The
1318: connected logarithm removes every empty-support scalar once. This proves the stated uniform row in
1319: exactly the marked norm consumed downstream.

1483: derivatives of the geometric recursion and hence (1 − m∗ )−5 . Lemma 3.29 ensures that this tree sum is
1484: over a partition of the exact histories, not a duplicated majorant. Every sum is rooted, so no volume

1606: Proof. Differentiate the BKAR functional along a tree. A hard-core derivative costs one overlap bond
1607: and no field derivative. A covariance derivative costs one covariance connector and one field derivative at
1608: each endpoint. If di covariance edges meet vertex i, the analytic row contributes di !σ −di Aαi ,N .
1609:    The Gram lemma bounds every auxiliary Gaussian regulator moment uniformly in the replica family.
1610: Summing the choices of hard-core versus covariance derivatives costs only 2n−1 . The Pruefer lemma
1611: shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
1612: an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
1613: in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
1614: exponential constant depending on N .

1601: for every auxiliary replica family, and that the parent-normalized rooted tree link constant, after the fixed
1602: exponential losses and the factor σ −2 , is strictly below one.
1603:    Then the normalized connected logarithm, summed with its exact 1/n!, converges absolutely in a
1604: weakened rooted support norm and is Fréchet-C N in the declared structural marks, uniformly in volume,

1606: Proof. Differentiate the BKAR functional along a tree. A hard-core derivative costs one overlap bond
1607: and no field derivative. A covariance derivative costs one covariance connector and one field derivative at
1608: each endpoint. If di covariance edges meet vertex i, the analytic row contributes di !σ −di Aαi ,N .
1609:    The Gram lemma bounds every auxiliary Gaussian regulator moment uniformly in the replica family.
1610: Summing the choices of hard-core versus covariance derivatives costs only 2n−1 . The Pruefer lemma
1611: shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
1612: an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
1613: in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
1614: exponential constant depending on N .

1603:    Then the normalized connected logarithm, summed with its exact 1/n!, converges absolutely in a
1604: weakened rooted support norm and is Fréchet-C N in the declared structural marks, uniformly in volume,
1605: replica number, forest parameters, and previous RG depth.
1606: Proof. Differentiate the BKAR functional along a tree. A hard-core derivative costs one overlap bond
1607: and no field derivative. A covariance derivative costs one covariance connector and one field derivative at
1608: each endpoint. If di covariance edges meet vertex i, the analytic row contributes di !σ −di Aαi ,N .
1609:    The Gram lemma bounds every auxiliary Gaussian regulator moment uniformly in the replica family.
1610: Summing the choices of hard-core versus covariance derivatives costs only 2n−1 . The Pruefer lemma
1611: shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
1612: an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
1613: in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
1614: exponential constant depending on N .

1729: 4.3    Gaussian reference and optical covariance
1730: This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
1731: and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1732: 1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
1733: 2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
1734: The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
1735: gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
1736: inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
1737: activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

2134:     Let JD (Y ) be the finite-dimensional space of gauge-complete, signed-hypercubic covariant, Wick-local
2135: jets of declared engineering degree at most D, including the extraction sector and the stable jet sector.
2136: Lemma 4.20 (Discrete covariant localization). There is a projector
2137: 
2138:                                           LocD,Y : FY 7−→ JD (Y )
2139: 
2140: with the following properties.
2141: 1. Loc2D,Y = LocD,Y , and it is exact on JD (Y ).
2142: 2. It commutes with gauge transformations, signed-hypercubic symmetries, and aligned reflection.
2143: 3. For 0 ≤ m ≤ 3,
2144:                                 ∥ LocD,Y FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD ∥FY ∥[m] .
2145: 4. If LocD,Y FY = 0, then after canonical scale-L rescaling,
2146: 
2147:                                  ∥TL FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD L3−D ∥FY ∥[m] .
2148: 
2149:   The estimate includes both the nonlocal output and its newly generated local projection:
2150: 
2151:                 ∥ LocD TL FY ∥[m] + ∥(1 − LocD )TL FY ∥[m] ≤ CD (1 + τ (Y ))pD L3−D ∥FY ∥[m] .
2152: 
2153: Proof. On each finite local template, take the gauge-complete Taylor expansion in normal coordinates
2154: and scale-dependent Wick monomials, transport all coefficients to the canonical root, and average over
2155: the finite stabilizer. The dual coefficient functionals use finitely many derivatives and finite-difference
2156: 
2157: 
2158:                                                      32
2159: 
2160: moments. Transport along a tree of radius τ (Y ) costs at most a polynomial in 1 + τ (Y ), proving the
2161: third row.
2162:    If LocD,Y FY = 0, every declared Taylor coefficient through degree D vanishes. The integral form of
2163: the covariant Taylor remainder begins at degree D + 1. Canonical rescaling gives one root-preimage factor
2164: L4 and L−(D+1) from the smallest omitted engineering degree. Thus the gain is
2165: 
2166:                                               L4−(D+1) = L3−D .
2167: 
2168: The regular linearized block kernel is analytic on the fixed tube and bounded through the three active
2169: marked rows, so integration and the local coefficient functionals preserve this remainder estimate. Applying
2170: the projector after integration proves the displayed localization-commutator bound; in particular, a
2171: remainder cannot feed the finite local block without paying the same L3−D factor.

1480:    Dominate each connected bipartite incidence graph by a rooted spanning tree rooted at a bad core.
1481: The zero-order marked product estimate has a cubic vertex polynomial and gives (1 − m∗ )−4 . A Frechet
1482: derivative marks one inherited vertex; together with three structural marks this gives the first four
1483: derivatives of the geometric recursion and hence (1 − m∗ )−5 . Lemma 3.29 ensures that this tree sum is
1484: over a partition of the exact histories, not a duplicated majorant. Every sum is rooted, so no volume
1485: factor occurs.
